### I-49 (S2) Most of the Figure 1B interaction rests on five libraries with a raised overall methylation level, two of them untreated; inside the heat cohort every diploid library departs from a profile that the triploids share with most control libraries

**Rating.** S2. The issue was rated S1 when it was written on 2026-11-19 and lowered the same day, after a check of its text. The ground then given, that passages of the Discussion read Figure 1B as a ploidy-specific response to heat, was wrong: no passage of the Discussion cites Figure 1B or percent CpG methylation. The interaction is reported in Results 3.1 only (F(1,18) = 14.49, with the heat-treated diploids named as the group with the highest CpG methylation), and the statements on global methylation in Discussion 4.5 and in the Conclusions rest on the ELISA (Figure 1A; I-10, I-12). By the definitions at the top of this file, an issue that changes how a reported result should be read, and on which no stated conclusion depends, is S2: most of the Figure 1B interaction rests on five libraries, two of them from untreated animals. That the interaction cannot be attributed to heat is part of I-01, which is S1. The DML lists of Results 3.2 to 3.5 do not depend on those five libraries; what removes their basis is I-01, I-02 and I-06, and for them this issue only describes what the libraries of the heat cohort look like. The authors may judge differently: the Figure 1B interaction is the only significant test of a ploidy by treatment interaction that the draft reports (the interaction of the ELISA has p = 0.058), and if they regard it as a main result the rating is S1.

**Where.** Results 3.1 and Figure 1B (percent CpG methylation and its ploidy by heat interaction); the reading of the DML lists as ploidy-specific responses to heat in Results 3.2 to 3.5, Discussion 4.3, 4.6 and 4.7, the Conclusions, and the section headed "3.5 Ploidy-Dependent Methylation Responses to Heat Stress" after the Conclusions; the analyses of I-01, I-02, I-06 and I-07. The draft's ploidy and heat lists themselves do not depend on the five libraries described here (see "Why it matters").

**What was done.** `06_diagnostics/library_methylation_level.R` describes the 22 libraries one by one and the contrasts between groups of them. It tests nothing at single CpGs. Tables are in `06_diagnostics/library_methylation_level/`. Numbers are legacy mode (480,579 CpGs); corrected mode gives the same reading and is in the same tables. A second agent then tried to refute every statement of the first version of this issue with its own code. It reproduced the numbers, withdrew one reading and narrowed eleven (see the corrections table). Its scripts and outputs are in `07_documentation/agent_workflows/verification_scripts/S2-I49-evidence-and-alternatives/`; where a statement below cites a file of that folder, the number was computed once by that agent and is not yet written by a script of the repository.

**Evidence (computed).**

1. *Five libraries have a higher overall methylation level than the other 17.* Genome-wide CpG methylation (Bismark) runs from 9.46% to 11.61%. Five libraries lie between 11.02% and 11.61%; the other 17 lie between 9.46% and 10.41% (`library_level.csv`). Over the 65,480 eligible CpGs, the five average 49% to 54% methylation and the other 17 average 38% to 44%.

   | Library | Animal | Group | Genome-wide CpG methylation | Mean over eligible CpGs |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | zr3534_3 | D13 | diploid, heat | 11.55% | 54.2% |
   | zr3534_4 | D19 (acute heat shock) | diploid, heat | 11.02% | 49.2% |
   | zr3534_5 | D20 (acute heat shock) | diploid, heat | 11.09% | 49.7% |
   | zr3644_14 | not recorded | triploid, control | 11.61% | 54.3% |
   | zr3644_15 | not recorded | triploid, control | 11.04% | 49.7% |

   They are the five highest of the 22, found by a rule that ignores the group labels: the libraries above the largest gap in the sorted genome-wide values (10.41% to 11.02%, 0.61 points). The same five lie above the largest gap in the mean over eligible CpGs, and k-means with two clusters, a two-component normal mixture and Tukey's fence pick the same five. They are the upper end of a skewed distribution more than a class of their own. The second largest gap (0.46 points) lies inside the five, between zr3534_5 (11.09%) and zr3534_3 (11.55%). The 12 libraries of project zr3644 that are not part of this study reach 10.44%, 10.61% and 10.70% (`03_input_files/bismark_reports/061021-big_bismark_summary_report.txt`), so that among the 24 libraries of that project the largest gap (0.58 points) sets apart zr3644_14 alone and zr3644_15 stands 0.34 points above the next library. A largest gap of 0.61 points or more occurs in 18% to 35% of samples of 22 drawn from a normal or a shifted log-normal distribution fitted to the 22 values (`05_alt4_gap_rule.out` in the folder named above).
   The genome-wide level correlates 0.93 with the first principal component of the tested CpGs (step 04; 9.5% of the variance) and 0.97 with the mean over the eligible CpGs (`level_correlations.csv`). Both correlations are those of the five against the other 17: among the 17 libraries that are not high the level correlates 0.58 with the first component and 0.78 with the mean over the eligible CpGs (Spearman 0.41 and 0.47), and 0.22 and 0.57 without zr3644_1, the lowest library (`09_summaries.out`). The first component is the contrast of the five with the rest, not a graded axis.

2. *Neither incomplete bisulfite conversion nor the amount of sequencing explains the level.* Methylation in CHH context, a common measure of non-conversion where no value of an unmethylated spike-in is at hand (Lea et al. 2017, Nat Ecol Evol 1:1074, doi:10.1038/s41559-017-0229-0; Olova et al. 2018, Genome Biol 19:33, doi:10.1186/s13059-018-1408-2) and an upper bound on it in this species, in which more than 99% of the methylated cytosines are in CpG context (mantle of two animals; Wang et al. 2014, BMC Genomics 15:1119, doi:10.1186/1471-2164-15-1119), is 0.47% to 0.53% in all 22 libraries, and in CHG context 0.44% to 0.50% (`04_output/02_alignment_qc/tables/bismark_by_library.csv`). A difference of 0.05 points in non-conversion moves CpG methylation by at most 0.05 points; the five high libraries exceed the other 17 by 0.6 to 2.2 points (11.26% against 10.10% on average; `library_level.csv`). Two further observations point the same way. The 363 tested CpGs of the mitochondrial genome, which in mammals carries no CpG methylation or nearly none (sources in I-01; in bivalves the two studies that sequenced it, on male gametes of C. gigas and on gonad of a mussel, found the same, and none is on gill), are no more methylated in the high libraries than in the others of their cohort (heat cohort: 0.32% to 0.42% in the three high libraries, 0.31% to 0.58% in the other seven; control cohort: 2.4% and 2.9% in the two high libraries, 2.0% to 3.6% in the other ten; the difference between the cohorts at these CpGs is taken up in I-01). In the control cohort the mitochondrial rate is four to seven times the nuclear non-CpG rate, so there, on the reading of I-01, which is an inference, it measures the conversion of mitochondrial DNA and not that of nuclear DNA (I-01); the comparison within cohort is not affected by this. And the excess of the high libraries over the others of their cohort is largest where methylation is intermediate (7 to 9 points at CpGs between 10% and 70%) and smallest at unmethylated CpGs (0.1 to 0.2 points at CpGs at or below 1%), where unconverted cytosines would add most (`02_alt1_artifacts.out`). On the logit scale the excess is close to a constant shift (an odds ratio of about 1.4 from CpGs under 1% to CpGs at 90%), which fits one factor acting on all CpGs.
   Nor does the level follow the amount or quality of sequencing. Within each cohort it is unrelated to the number of read pairs (Spearman 0.35 in the heat cohort, -0.01 in the control cohort), the duplication rate (0.14, -0.33), the mapping efficiency (-0.37, 0.33), the number of CpGs with 10x coverage (0.42, -0.07) and the mean coverage at the tested CpGs (-0.19, -0.18); none of 13 such quantities reaches p = 0.05 in either cohort (`02_level_vs_qc.csv`). The same five libraries are the highest before deduplication (`04_output/03_global_methylation/tables/pct_mCpG_by_library.csv`).
   Non-CpG methylation does rise with the level (CHH: Spearman 0.82 in the heat cohort and 0.83 in the control cohort; `level_correlations.csv`). In the heat cohort part of this comes from the two acute heat shock triploids, zr3534_9 and zr3534_10, whose CHH methylation (0.48% and 0.47%) lies below the 0.51% to 0.53% of the other eight; without them the correlation is 0.64 (p = 0.10). The five high libraries exceed the other 17 by 0.02 points in CHH methylation on average, so whatever raises the level of a library leaves at most a small trace in the calls made outside CpG context, and calls at positions that are CpG in the animal but not in the reference could account for it.
   Whether the cause is biological (animals or tissue samples that are more methylated, or, for the three heat diploids, a response to the treatment) or lies in library preparation cannot be decided from these data.
   Published work gives candidates of both kinds. *Library preparation.* Fragments that carry unmethylated cytosines are lost preferentially during bisulfite treatment, and amplification builds on that loss, so that reads from methylated DNA are over-represented and the absolute level is overestimated by an amount that depends on the conversion protocol, the polymerase and the number of PCR cycles (Ji et al. 2014, Front Genet 5:341, doi:10.3389/fgene.2014.00341; Olova et al. 2018, Genome Biol 19:33, doi:10.1186/s13059-018-1408-2, who conclude that "changes of up to 20% in DNA methylation can be purely technical"). If reads from methylated molecules were over-represented by the same factor everywhere, such a bias would multiply the odds of methylation at every CpG, which is the constant shift on the logit scale described above; that is a reading of this log, not a result of either study (Olova et al. find the differences between protocols largest at intermediate methylation, as a constant factor on the odds also gives, and dependent on the cytosine content of the region), and according to Olova et al. the same loss and amplification favour poorly converted reads, so that methylation outside CpGs is overestimated as well. The checks of this item therefore do not exclude it: the CHH methylation of the five high libraries exceeds that of the others by 0.02 points, which is the direction that such a bias gives, at a size that the sources do not let one predict. Olova et al. compared seven library protocols, mostly on mouse embryonic stem cells, and Ji et al. compared three polymerases and 4, 8 and 15 PCR cycles within one protocol on Arabidopsis thaliana, where the enrichment grew with the cycles for one polymerase (Pfu Turbo Cx) and not for another (Kapa HiFi Uracil+), without measuring its effect on the methylation level; technical replicates of one protocol differed little in Olova et al., and no report of a difference of this size between libraries of one kit and one run was found. The efficiency of conversion can also differ between samples or groups of samples prepared together, and in the one comparison the authors show, a baboon RRBS data set, the estimates from spike-in DNA, from CHH and CHG sites and from read ends roughly agreed and the spike-in seemed to capture a batch effect of sample preparation best (Lea et al. 2017, Nat Ecol Evol 1:1074, doi:10.1038/s41559-017-0229-0), which is the reason for asking for the values of the E. coli amplicons below. *The animal.* In adductor muscle of C. gigas, females have a globally lower methylation than males (Teng et al. 2025, Mar Biotechnol 27:31, doi:10.1007/s10126-024-10409-6); in C. gigas gonad the male is the more methylated sex (Sun, Li and Yu 2022, Gene 820:146260, doi:10.1016/j.gene.2022.146260); and in gill of the flat oyster Ostrea edulis methylation differs between sex phenotypes at single regions and without a common direction: the differences are most numerous between hermaphrodite stages, males and females differ at 239 regions, 125 more methylated in males and 114 in females, and no overall level by sex is reported (Valk et al. 2025, BMC Genomics 26:567, doi:10.1186/s12864-025-11736-1; 35 wild animals of five sex phenotypes, nanopore sequencing). The one study of gill therefore gives no precedent for a sex difference in the overall level of that tissue. The sample sheet (`03_input_files/metadata/wgbs_libraries.csv`) records no sex, and the author confirms that sex was not recorded (2026-11-19), so this cannot be examined. Methylation also follows family (see "Why it matters"). (Read in full: Olova et al., Lea et al., and Ji et al. in the second reading of 2026-11-19; Valk et al. also in that reading; in the abstract only: Sun et al. and the article of Teng et al. The preprint of Teng et al., read in full, names C. gigas, with parents and offspring of two full-sib families, sample means of CpG methylation of 11% to 14%, the sexes apart on the first principal component and the families on the second; it shows the difference between the sexes in a figure and gives no number for it (Teng et al. 2024, Research Square, doi:10.21203/rs.3.rs-3866716/v1). Valk et al. report no overall level by sex.)

3. *Figure 1B is mostly these five libraries.* The ploidy by heat interaction in percent CpG methylation (F(1,18) = 14.49 in the draft; I-01) comes mostly from the diploid heat group holding three of the five and the triploid control group holding the other two: the interaction contrast (ploidy difference of the heat cohort minus that of the control cohort) is -1.50 points with all 22 libraries and -0.57 points with the five set aside. The smaller contrast is still nominally significant in the deduplicated genome-wide values of `library_level.csv` (F(1,13) = 6.79, p = 0.022; 32 of the 4,410 regroupings of the 17 libraries within cohort give a contrast as large), because D11 and D12 lie above all five heat triploids (10.24% and 10.21% against 9.81% to 10.19%) and the four other control triploids lie above most control diploids (10.18% to 10.41% against 9.46% to 10.23%; exact Wilcoxon p = 0.038). The draft's Figure 1B and its F(1,18) = 14.49 use the counts before deduplication (I-13; column `pct_mCpG_predup` of `04_output/03_global_methylation/tables/pct_mCpG_by_library.csv`), as do the two differences quoted in I-01. On those counts the interaction contrast is -1.52 points with all 22 libraries and -0.54 points with the five set aside, and the smaller contrast is at the edge of nominal significance (F(1,13) = 4.77, p = 0.048; F(1,13) = 4.69, p = 0.0495 on the arcsine square root scale that the draft used; `06_figure1b_dedup.log` in `07_documentation/agent_workflows/verification_scripts/S4-updates-and-cross-file/`; computed once by that check, not yet written by a script of the repository). It is not significant in the mean over the eligible CpGs (F(1,13) = 2.78, p = 0.12). Setting the five aside was decided after seeing the data, so these values are exploratory (`library_level.csv`; `01_numbers.out` and `10_followups.out`).
   Exact Wilcoxon tests of the genome-wide level with all libraries (`level_group_tests.csv`): diploid against triploid inside the heat cohort p = 0.008 (every diploid above every triploid, by 0.02 points at the closest pair); inside the control cohort p = 0.009 in the opposite direction (five of the six triploids above every diploid); heat cohort against control cohort p = 0.82; diploid against triploid over all 22 libraries p = 0.90.

4. *Inside each cohort the contrast between the ploidy groups follows the high libraries.* CpGs at which the read-pooled difference exceeds 20 points, by sign (`contrast_direction.csv`):

   | Contrast | CpGs above 20 points | Triploid higher (or heat cohort higher) | Diploid higher (or control cohort higher) | Median difference at eligible CpGs |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | Ploidy, all 22 libraries (the draft's ploidy contrast) | 1,038 | 442 | 596 | -1.2 |
   | Cohort, all 22 libraries (the draft's heat contrast) | 2,352 | 1,150 | 1,202 | +0.4 |
   | Ploidy inside the heat cohort | 13,028 | 1,915 | 11,113 (85%) | -5.7 |
   | Ploidy inside the control cohort | 3,349 | 2,688 (80%) | 661 | +3.6 |
   | Interaction (heat-cohort ploidy difference minus control-cohort ploidy difference) | 23,503 | 2,876 | 20,627 (88%) | -8.9 |

   In the interaction row the two columns hold the CpGs at which the ploidy difference (triploid minus diploid) of the heat cohort is more than 20 points above, or more than 20 points below, that of the control cohort. Inside each cohort the differences run in one direction, the direction of the group that holds the high libraries, and the two cohorts run in opposite directions. The share of 85% in one direction is common to the regroupings of the ten heat libraries that keep the three high libraries together (median 88% over those 21 regroupings and their mirror images, 62% over the other 210; `07_alt678_elisa_hypothesis.out`). Across CpGs the ploidy difference of the heat cohort and that of the control cohort correlate negatively: Pearson -0.30 and Spearman -0.10 over all tested CpGs, Pearson -0.21 and Spearman -0.25 over the eligible ones (`contrast_agreement.csv`). A consistent effect of triploidy would add a positive correlation.
   The CpGs involved are the same in both cohorts. The slope of each CpG's methylation on the genome-wide level was estimated among the libraries of one cohort and set against the ploidy difference of the other cohort, so that no library is on both sides. Among the eligible CpGs in the top tenth of the slope estimated in the control cohort, 47% exceed 20 points with the diploids higher in the heat cohort and 1% with the triploids higher; in tenths 3 to 6, 6% to 9% and 3%; in the bottom tenth, 10% and 6%. In the other direction the association is weaker: in the top tenth of the slope estimated in the heat cohort, 7% exceed 20 points with the triploids higher in the control cohort and 0.5% with the diploids higher; in tenths 3 to 6, 2% to 3% and 1% (`level_slope_deciles.csv`). About half of the eligible CpGs that the heat-cohort contrast lists with the diploids higher lie in the top two tenths of the slope estimated in the control cohort (51% of 10,189, where 20% would lie if the two were unrelated).
   The slope estimated in the control cohort is in effect the contrast between that cohort's two high libraries and its other ten. With the level values permuted among the 12 control libraries, the top tenth of the slope holds a median of 15% of CpGs with the diploids higher in the heat cohort (500 permutations; 5th to 95th percentile 11% to 42%; 6 reach the observed 47%), about the 16% of all eligible CpGs, and the asymmetry returns only when a large value falls on zr3644_14 (median 38% in the 88 permutations that give it one of the two largest values, 15% and 12% in the others). So the association is not a by-product of variable CpGs having both large slopes and large differences. It is also not a graded dependence on the level: among the ten control libraries that are not high the slope predicts nothing (15% in the top tenth; permutation range 12% to 18%). What the analysis shows is that the CpGs at which zr3644_14 and zr3644_15 exceed the other control libraries are the CpGs at which the heat diploids exceed the heat triploids (`06_alt5_slope_permutation.out` and `10_followups.out`).

5. *With the five libraries set aside* (exploratory: the rule that finds them ignores the labels, but setting them aside was decided after seeing the data). Number of CpGs above 20 points for the observed ploidy grouping, against every regrouping of the same libraries into groups of the same sizes (`relabelling_reference.csv`):

   | Comparison | Observed | Median of all regroupings | Largest other regrouping | Regroupings at or above the observed |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | Heat cohort, all ten (5 diploid, 5 triploid) | 13,028 | 4,018.5 | 12,822 | 2 of 252 (the observed one and its mirror image) |
   | Control cohort, all twelve (6, 6) | 3,349 | 2,912.5 | 4,749 | 242 of 924 |
   | Heat cohort without its three high libraries (2, 5) | 10,732 | 7,234 | 8,737 | 1 of 21 |
   | Control cohort without its two high libraries (6, 4) | 3,190 | 3,713 | 5,806 | 179 of 210 |
   | Both cohorts without the five (8, 9), labels moved within cohort | 1,425 | 1,318 | 2,612 | 1,552 of 4,410 |

   The observed grouping of the ten heat libraries leads the next one, in which D11 and T11 change places, by 206 CpGs (13,028 against 12,822; `relabelling_small_sets.csv`), so "2 of 252" mostly restates where the three high libraries are. In the control cohort the number of CpGs above 20 points does not set the ploidy grouping apart from other groupings, with or without the two high libraries. The overall level does differ between its diploids and triploids (item 3), also among the ten libraries that are not high (10.18% to 10.41% in the four triploids against 9.46% to 10.23% in the six diploids, exact Wilcoxon p = 0.038; `library_level.csv`). In the heat cohort the two diploids with an ordinary level (D11, D12) still differ from the five triploids at more CpGs than any other pair of those seven libraries does; 1 of 21 is the smallest value this comparison can give. The reading does not depend on which gap defines the high libraries: cutting at the second largest gap, which sets aside only zr3534_3 and zr3644_14, gives 9,178 CpGs against a median of 4,462 in the heat cohort (1 of 126 regroupings at or above) and 2,810 against 2,957.5 in the control cohort (290 of 462; `out/alternative_gap_cut.log` in `07_documentation/agent_workflows/verification_scripts/S3-code-review-library-level-r2/`; computed once by the review of the script, not yet written by it). That remaining difference has no preferred direction (59% of the CpGs have the diploids higher, against 85% with all ten libraries; the other 20 pairs give 51% to 61%). With the five set aside, the ploidy differences of the two cohorts are unrelated across CpGs (Pearson -0.04 over all tested CpGs, -0.03 over the eligible ones; of 923 CpGs above 20 points in both, 407 have the same sign; `contrast_agreement.csv`).

6. *Inside the heat cohort the five triploids share one profile, which is also that of most control libraries; the five diploids do not.* The ten pairwise correlations among the heat triploids (0.923 to 0.937) are the ten highest of the 45 in the cohort; the other pairs lie between 0.875 and 0.913 (step 04, `library_correlation_10x_legacy.csv`). Their mean pairwise correlation, 0.929, is the highest of all 252 subsets of five (`pair_similarity.csv`). The rank depends on the statistic: with the median instead of the mean, 6 of the 252 subsets reach the value of the triploids, because five subsets that hold four of the five triploids have exactly the same median (found by the review of the script and recomputed by the session from `04_output/04_cpg_coverage/tables/library_correlation_10x_legacy.csv`; not yet written by the script). Read depth does not produce this: the triploid libraries have no more reads at the tested CpGs than the diploid ones (34.8 against 35.3 per CpG on average), and with every heat library thinned to the same number of reads at each CpG the triploids remain the most similar of the 252 subsets in each of eight draws. The level does not produce it either: among the seven heat libraries that are not high the ten triploid pairs hold ranks 1 to 10 of 21, and with the level removed by slopes estimated in the control cohort the triploid subset is still the most similar of 252 (`03_alt23_similarity.out`).
   The five diploids are no more similar to each other (0.898) than to the triploids (0.898); D11 and D12, the two diploids with an ordinary level, are the least similar pair among the seven libraries of the cohort that are not high (0.906, below each of their correlations with a triploid, 0.907 to 0.913). In the control cohort similarity does not follow ploidy: 0.912 among diploids, 0.908 among triploids, 0.908 between (`pair_similarity.csv`). These values cannot be set beside the 0.929 of the heat triploids, because the control libraries have fewer reads and a correlation falls with sampling noise. With every library thinned to the same number of reads at each CpG, the five heat triploids correlate at 0.903 on average, the ten control libraries that are not high at 0.901, and seven of these (zr3644_3, 4, 5, 6, 13, 16 and 18, of both ploidies) at 0.916. The heat triploids are therefore as similar to each other as ordinary libraries of the control cohort are, and they resemble those libraries too: each heat triploid correlates more with every one of the 12 control libraries than D11 or D12 does (120 of 120 comparisons in `library_correlation_10x_legacy.csv`), and more with the ten control libraries that are not high (0.913 to 0.917 on average) than with D11 and D12 (0.907 to 0.913). What is particular to the heat cohort is that all five diploids lie outside this common profile, three by their level and two in ways they do not share with each other, while all five triploids lie inside it (`04_cross_cohort_similarity.out` and `09_summaries.out`). A profile shared with animals of another experiment does not call for one cross or one processing batch among the triploids. The open question is what sets the five diploids of the heat experiment apart: their source, the handling of their libraries, or their response to heat.
   Among the 17 libraries that are not high, the four that resemble the others least are the libraries numbered 1 and 2 of each project: zr3644_1, zr3534_2 (D12), zr3534_1 (D11) and zr3644_2 (mean correlation with the other 16 at equal coverage 0.874 to 0.886, against 0.890 to 0.904 for the other 13; exact Wilcoxon p = 0.0008, a comparison made after seeing the data; `11_library_order.out`). Something tied to the position of a library in its project would therefore account for D11 and D12 as well as ploidy or heat does. In both projects the library numbers run diploids first and triploids second (zr3534_1 to 5 and 6 to 10; zr3644_1 to 6 and 13 to 18), so anything that follows the order of processing is aligned with ploidy.

7. *Some of the CpGs that pass the corrected test show one group apart.* Of the 1,093 CpGs that separate the ploidy groups of the heat cohort with the overdispersion correction (I-06), 36 have all five heat-cohort triploids outside the range of the other 17 libraries and 16 have all five heat-cohort diploids outside it; the other 1,041 show no group apart (`corrected_dml_group_apart.csv`). The criterion is weak (at 24 of the 52 the group is separated from the nearest other library by less than 3.5 points; `corrected_dml_library_values.csv`, column `margin_of_group_apart_points`), and only the triploids are set apart more often than an arbitrary set of five heat libraries is: over all 480,579 tested CpGs they lie outside the range of the other 17 libraries at 98 CpGs, where the other 250 sets of five heat libraries give a median of 14 and at most 59, and the five diploids do so at 34 CpGs, a number that 13 of the other 250 sets reach (I-08, update). This does not conflict with item 6, where the triploids are the group inside the common profile: item 6 is about the similarity of whole libraries over 480,579 CpGs, and this is about complete separation at 98 of them, which alleles shared within one group would give without making its libraries unlike the others overall. At NC_047565.1:35,030,701, the CpG with the smallest p-value in the two corrected lists of the draft's contrasts, all five heat-cohort triploids are 35.5% to 62.5% methylated and the other 17 libraries are at 0% to 7% (`corrected_dml_library_values.csv`); the tested CpG 16 bp away shows the same (34% to 80% against 0% to 6%; `04_output/05_differential_methylation/tables/united_cpg_legacy.csv.gz`). An allele shared by related animals gives this pattern, and so would a response to heat confined to the triploids; the alignments are needed to tell them apart (I-06, item 6, and I-08). If the triploids were made by mating tetraploid males with diploid females, two of their three chromosome sets come from a tetraploid stock, and the genetic diversity of such stocks is generally limited (sources under "Why it matters"), and alleles shared among the triploids of one batch are then expected.

**Why it matters.**
- Inside the heat cohort the difference between diploids and triploids consists, in these data, of two things: (a) a higher overall methylation level in the diploids, most of which sits in three libraries, and (b) five triploid libraries that resemble each other and most control libraries, against five diploid libraries that each lie outside that common profile. Neither has been shown to be a property of ploidy or an effect of heat, and neither is excluded as one. A response to heat confined to the diploids, which is what the draft proposes, would produce both; so would five diploid animals that differed from the others before the treatment, or libraries that differ through their preparation. The WGBS data cannot tell these apart, because the control animals of the heat experiment were not sequenced (I-01). What they do show is that (a) is not specific to heat: two of the six untreated triploids of the control cohort, which belongs to another experiment (whether its animals share a source with those of the heat experiment is not known; I-08, I-36), carry the same raised level at the same CpGs (item 4), so that the level difference between ploidy groups has the opposite sign there, and libraries with a raised level are not demonstrably more frequent after heat (3 of 10 heat libraries against 2 of the 12 control libraries, Fisher exact p = 0.62; against 2 of the 24 libraries of project zr3644, p = 0.14).
- The level does not explain the draft's own DML lists. The draft's two contrasts are balanced for the five high libraries (three and two on either side; the level differs by -0.03 points between the ploidy groups and by +0.06 points between the cohorts, `level_group_tests.csv`), and level-sensitive CpGs cancel in them: 3% of the draft's ploidy DML and 3% of its heat DML lie in the top tenth of the slope on the level, where 10% of the eligible CpGs lie by construction, and without the five libraries 61% of the 1,038 ploidy CpGs and 76% of the 2,352 cohort CpGs stay above 20 points with the same sign, as many as when any other three heat diploids and two control triploids are left out (57% to 74% and 65% to 81%; `07_alt678_elisa_hypothesis.out` and `08_title_scope.out`). Why DML lie where methylation is intermediate and variable among animals, and why the functions assigned to them reflect which genes have such CpGs, is explained in I-41 and I-03: the 20-point rule selects such CpGs under any grouping. What the level does explain is the direction of the contrasts inside each cohort and of the interaction (item 4), and most of the interaction of Figure 1B (item 3).
- The ELISA of the heat experiment reports a difference of the same kind as (a): 4.0% 5-mC in heat-treated diploids against 2.2% in heat-treated triploids, and 1.7% in both control groups (`04_output/03_global_methylation/tables/group_summary.csv`; limits of the assay in I-10 and I-12). Four of the eight values of the heat-treated diploids (D13 to D16) lie above the highest standard used in the fit and are extrapolated (`elisa_percent_5mC.csv`), so 4.0% is not a measured mean; with the four set to the value of that standard the mean is 3.3%. The direction does not depend on them: five of the eight heat-treated diploids exceed every heat-treated triploid (exact Wilcoxon p = 0.050). The two assays agree in direction for the heat-treated animals and not in size: the heat-treated diploids exceed the heat-treated triploids by 80% in the ELISA and by 9% in genome-wide CpG methylation. The ELISA is the only methylation measurement that includes the control animals of the heat experiment, and therefore the only evidence for an effect of heat on overall methylation in diploids that is free of the confound of I-01. The WGBS can neither confirm nor contradict it, for two reasons. It has no control animals from the heat experiment, and in the other experiment two of six untreated triploids are as high as the three high heat-treated diploids, so libraries with a high level occur without heat. And only six animals have both an ELISA value and a WGBS library (D11 to D13, T11 to T13): the two measurements correlate at 0.54 (Spearman, p = 0.30), and at 0.20 without D13, which is the highest in both and one of the four extrapolated ELISA values. D19 and D20, two of the three high heat-treated diploids, have no ELISA value.
- What published work says about the alternatives (added 2026-11-19 by the check of the citations). *Family.* In an experiment that set family against environment in eastern oysters, methylation followed family more than environment: among 80 animals (Crassostrea virginica; gill; reduced representation bisulfite sequencing; 73 with usable methylation data) of six full-sib families (two dams by three sires, assigned afterwards from genotypes and unevenly spread over the sites) grown for 14 months at two sites that differ in salinity, methylation patterns "were largely driven by genetic differences among families", with nearly two orders of magnitude more genes differentially methylated between families than between sites (Johnson, Sirovy and Kelly 2022, J Anim Ecol 91:1135, doi:10.1111/1365-2656.13645). In C. gigas methylation patterns are transmitted from parents to offspring and differ between families (Jiang et al. 2016, Mar Biotechnol 18:124, doi:10.1007/s10126-015-9674-4; Olson and Roberts 2014, bioRxiv, doi:10.1101/012831, a preprint; Teng et al. 2025, cited in item 2), and in the Olympia oyster pairwise methylation distance correlates with genetic distance (Pearson 0.27; Silliman et al. 2023, Genome Biol Evol 15:evad013, doi:10.1093/gbe/evad013). Two groups of animals from different crosses are therefore expected to differ at many CpGs whatever their ploidy or treatment. A study of wild animals points the other way at the level of populations: among 80 eastern oysters of four estuaries (gill), methylation diverged almost five times as much as allele frequencies (mean PST 0.10 against mean FST 0.023), which the authors read as likely environmental, and population-specific methylation was "not directly associated with single nucleotide polymorphisms", although they also see "some influence of genetic structure on methylation state" (Johnson and Kelly 2020, Evol Appl 13:945, doi:10.1111/eva.12912). *Ploidy.* Two published comparisons of diploid and triploid C. gigas across the genome report no difference in the global level (methylation-sensitive amplified polymorphism; Jiang et al. 2016) and, in gonad, a decreased CpG methylation level in infertile triploids and the opposite in fertile ones, with overall profiles over gene and transposable regions like those of diploids (WGBS; Sun, Yu and Li 2022, Mar Biotechnol 24:18, doi:10.1007/s10126-021-10083-y). Sun, Yu and Li worked on gonad, and the abstract of Jiang et al. names no tissue; neither concerns a response to heat, and no comparison in another bivalve was found. They give no precedent for a ploidy difference in overall level, in ctenidium or in any other somatic tissue. *Independence.* Animals of one cross, or animals treated together in one container, are not independent replicates of a ploidy or of a treatment (Hurlbert 1984, Ecol Monogr 54:187, doi:10.2307/1942661; Lazic 2010, BMC Neurosci 11:5, doi:10.1186/1471-2202-11-5; Lazic and Essioux 2013, BMC Neurosci 14:37, doi:10.1186/1471-2202-14-37, for litters), and in bisulfite data relatedness that is aligned with the tested variable gives spurious associations (Lea, Tung and Zhou 2015, cited in I-06; Lea et al. 2017, cited in item 2). *How triploids are made.* Triploid oysters for aquaculture are mostly made by mating tetraploid males with diploid females, and in some places by direct induction in eggs of diploid crosses (Guo, DeBrosse and Allen 1996, Aquaculture 142:149, doi:10.1016/0044-8486(95)01243-5; Vignier, Adams and Lovatelli 2024, FAO Fisheries and Aquaculture Technical Paper 698, doi:10.4060/cd1852en). Two of the three chromosome sets of such a triploid come from a tetraploid stock whose genetic diversity "is generally limited" (Vignier et al. 2024); in one commercial tetraploid stock diversity dropped over two generations and the effective population size was below the census number (Miller et al. 2014, Aquaculture 433:318, doi:10.1016/j.aquaculture.2014.06.025; abstract), and a mated triploid cannot be a full sib of a diploid. Commercial triploid cohorts are mostly made by mass spawning of several parents, not as one cross, but the parents contribute unequally: in two such cohorts the effective population size was 34% to 49% below the census number (Miller et al. 2016, Aquac Res 47:1288, doi:10.1111/are.12588). How the triploids of the two experiments were made is not recorded. (Read in full: Silliman et al., Lazic 2010, Lea, Tung and Zhou, Lea et al., the FAO manual of Vignier et al. Read in full in the second reading of 2026-11-19: Johnson, Sirovy and Kelly; Johnson and Kelly; Lazic and Essioux. In the abstract only: Jiang et al., whose abstract names no tissue; Olson and Roberts; Sun, Yu and Li; Hurlbert; both papers of Miller et al.; the article of Teng et al., whose abstract does not name the species and whose preprint, read in full, names C. gigas (Teng et al. 2024, Research Square, doi:10.21203/rs.3.rs-3866716/v1). Not opened: Guo et al., cited for the method through the manual. What the abstract-only sources report on effect sizes and on numbers of parents is not known here, and the quotations from them are from their abstracts.)

**What would settle it.** WGBS of the control animals of the heat experiment (I-01, option 1); because libraries with a raised level occur in 2 of 12 untreated libraries here (2 of the 24 of project zr3644), that needs more than a few animals per group to show that the level rises with heat. The pedigree of the animals (I-36). Variant calls from the alignments (I-08); the same calls give an estimate of the pairwise relatedness of the animals, which tests whether the triploids, or the diploids, of the heat experiment are one family without the pedigree. Genotypes called from bisulfite reads carry error (Lea et al. 2017, cited in item 2), more so at the coverage of these libraries (6 to 12 reads on average over the genome) and in triploids, so the estimate separates full sibs from unrelated animals more safely than it grades relatedness. A second library from the DNA of one high and one ordinary animal of each cohort would tell a property of the animal from an artifact of library preparation. Without new data: show the individual libraries in Figure 1B, state that most of the interaction rests on five libraries, two of them untreated (interaction contrast -1.50 points with all 22 libraries and -0.57 points without the five in the deduplicated values; -1.52 and -0.54 on the counts before deduplication that the draft's figure uses), and do not interpret lists of loci.

**Needs from authors.**
- Source and crosses of the diploid and the triploid animals of each experiment. Were the triploids of the heat experiment one family? Did the diploids of the heat experiment come from the same stock as its triploids and as the animals of the control experiment? How were the triploids of each experiment produced: by mating tetraploid males with diploid females, or by induction in eggs of the cross that gave the diploids? How many sires and dams contributed to each of the four groups? (Author, 2026-11-19: not known at present; to be added when the information is found.)
- The order and batches in which the libraries were prepared, and whether the libraries numbered 1 and 2 of each project (zr3534_1, zr3534_2, zr3644_1, zr3644_2) were handled differently from the rest, for example as a first batch.
- Anything recorded about animals D11, D12, D13, D19 and D20 and about the animals behind zr3644_14 and zr3644_15: size, sex, gonad stage, condition, position in the sampling order, the time between the acute heat shock and sampling for D19 and D20, and the amount and quality of the DNA that went into each library.
- The bisulfite conversion efficiency of each library. The Methods of the draft say that E. coli amplicons were added to every sample to assess it; the per-library values from the sequencing provider, or the reads that map to those amplicons, would settle item 2 directly.
- Whether libraries zr3534_9 and zr3534_10 were converted or prepared separately from zr3534_1 to 8: their non-CpG methylation (CHH 0.48% and 0.47%, CHG 0.45% and 0.44%) lies below that of the other eight (CHH 0.51% to 0.53%, CHG 0.48% to 0.50%; `04_output/02_alignment_qc/tables/bismark_by_library.csv`).
- The sex of all 22 animals, if it was recorded or can be read from histology (item 2). (Author, 2026-11-19: not recorded.)
- For both projects: the method of DNA extraction, the amount of DNA that went into each library, the library kit and bisulfite conversion protocol, and the number of PCR cycles of each library. The sample sheet records the kit for the heat libraries only (`03_input_files/metadata/wgbs_libraries.csv`, column `library_kit`); absolute methylation levels depend on the conversion protocol, the polymerase and the number of cycles (item 2), and the conversion of mitochondrial DNA differs between the two projects (I-01). Methods 2.2 describe the extraction in the words of Venkataraman et al. 2022, where the DNA came from histology blocks (I-28): was the ctenidium tissue of either experiment fixed or embedded? (Author, 2026-11-19: neither; the ctenidia were flash frozen and stored at -80 C before extraction. If that holds for both experiments, fixation is not the cause of the difference between the projects in the conversion of mitochondrial DNA; the other details asked for here are still open.)
- Whether the 24 h emersion was applied to the heat-treated animals in one container and one run or in several, and how the control animals of that experiment were held meanwhile. If each treatment was applied once to one group of animals, the animals are subsamples of a single application of the treatment (Hurlbert 1984, cited under "Why it matters"), also for the ELISA (I-01, I-10). (Author, 2026-11-19: the 24 h heat emersion is not confirmed; all heat-treated animals count as one treatment group. The question stays open.)
- Whether tissue or DNA of the control animals of the heat experiment (D01 to D08, T01 to T08) remains.

---

## Added 2026-11-20

### I-50 (S3) The "transposable element" track is the whole RepeatMasker output, and it was read with a one-base shift

**Where.** Methods 2.3, the sentence on the location of transposable elements ("obtained from RepeatMasker using the NCBI RefSeq annotation (RefSeq: GCF_902806645.1)"); Results 3.1, last paragraph ("transposable elements (13.1%)" of the CpGs at 1x); Results 3.3, first paragraph ("transposable elements (18.4%)" of the CpGs at 10x; "transposable elements (14.9% and 16.0%; Figure 3A)" for ploidy-DML and heat-DML; "reduced representation in transposable elements (10.3%)" for interaction-DML) and second paragraph ("comparatively depleted for transposable elements (5.7%)" for the interaction-DML with contrasting responses); the category of that name in every bar of Figure 3A. These are rows M2.3-005, R3.1-043, R3.1-047, R3.3-004, R3.3-007, R3.3-026 to R3.3-029, R3.3-032, R3.3-033, R3.3-046 and R3.3-047 of `05_manuscript/manuscript_values.csv`. The Discussion and the Conclusions make no statement on transposable elements.

**Evidence (verified).** `03_input_files/genome_features/cgigas_uk_roslin_v1_rm.te.bed.gz` is columns 5 to 7 (sequence, begin, end) of NCBI's RepeatMasker output for the assembly, `GCF_902806645.1_cgigas_uk_roslin_v1_rm.out.gz`, without its three header lines: 781,312 records, identical line for line. The notebook that built the tracks does exactly that (`code/08-Generating-Genome-Feature-Tracks.ipynb` of the OSF archive, cells 64 to 67). Two things follow.
- *Content.* The file holds every repeat class: DNA transposons 380,683 records, simple repeats 165,597, Helitrons (class RC) 151,061, LINE 29,494, low complexity 21,815, LTR 19,774, unknown 9,973, satellites 1,461, SINE 1,391, rRNA 63. Simple repeats, low complexity, satellites and rRNA are 24.2% of the records and 4.0% of their bases. Of the 109,966 analysed CpGs that step 07 counts in the track in legacy mode, 3,697 (3.4%) overlap no DNA, RC, LTR, LINE or SINE record, 2,158 of them in a record of class unknown.
- *Coordinates.* RepeatMasker positions are 1-based closed: no record starts at 0 and 47 start at 1, and the first record, `(C)n` at 4109 to 4128 of NC_047559.1, is a run of exactly 20 C in the reference. The legacy notebook gave the file to `intersectBed` under its name `.bed` (`code/3_WGBS_DML_feature_location-new.ipynb`, cells 15 to 17 for the 22-library sets), which takes the start as 0-based, so every interval was read without its first base. The legacy counts (194 ploidy-DML, 463 heat-DML, 28 interaction loci in the track) are those of that reading.

**Effect.** Small in numbers; what is wrong is the name of the category and its description in the Methods.
- *Coordinates read as written*, legacy mode: 110,067 instead of 109,966 analysed CpGs in the track (101 more, each with its G on the first base of a record), 19,426 instead of 19,411 eligible CpGs, 465 instead of 463 heat DML; the 194 ploidy DML and the 28 loci in both lists do not change. None of the 56 shares of the kind Results 3.3 prints (overlaps of a track over the summed overlaps of eight tracks, I-18; seven sets) changes at one decimal, and the odds ratios of step 07 for the track change by less than 0.004. Corrected mode: 98,763 instead of 98,671 CpGs (92 more), 459 instead of 458 heat DML.
- *Transposable elements only* (classes DNA, RC, LTR, LINE and SINE; coordinates as the draft read them), legacy mode: 106,269 analysed CpGs instead of 109,966 (22.1% of loci instead of 22.9%), 189 ploidy DML instead of 194, 455 heat DML instead of 463, and the same 28 loci in both lists (8, 10 and 10 by class). As the draft computes its percentages, the track falls from 18.1% to 17.6% for the analysed CpGs (the draft prints 18.4%, from the workbook column of I-14), from 14.9% to 14.6% for the ploidy DML and from 16.0% to 15.7% for the heat DML; 10.3% and 5.7% for the interaction loci stay. Because every share is taken over the sum of eight tracks, other features rise a little: 12 of the 56 shares change at one decimal, none by more than 0.5 points (introns of the ploidy and heat DML 39.6% and 42.0% to 39.8% and 42.1%; CDS 28.6% and 27.6% to 28.7% and 27.7%).
- The direction of every statement stays under each reading: DML lie in the track less often than the CpGs they are compared with. With the five classes only, the odds ratios against all analysed CpGs are 0.78 (ploidy), 0.85 (heat) and 0.57 (both lists), against 0.77, 0.83 and 0.55 as run; against eligible CpGs 0.55, 0.59 and 0.40 against 0.54, 0.57 and 0.39. What such a comparison can show is the subject of I-41 and is not changed by this entry.
- The 13.1% of Results 3.1 was computed with the same file and the same reading; the table that traces it is the one of I-15, and it was not recomputed under the other readings.

**Status of the pipeline.** Nothing is corrected (scope decision of 2026-11-19). `read_feature_track()` reads the file as the legacy analysis did, in legacy and in corrected mode, so the legacy counts are reproduced (78 of 78 overlap counts in `06_diagnostics/legacy_check_results.csv`). What the track is, and the numbers above, are stated in `03_input_files/genome_features/README.md` (section "The repeat track"), in the headers of `02_R_functions/genomic_overlaps.R` and of step 07, and in the column `track_label` of the tables that are written by track. The track keeps the name `TE` in the tables, because the diagnostics and the values map select it by that name.

**Verification.** Found by the code review of steps 06 and 07 and confirmed by a second agent, which downloaded the RepeatMasker output from NCBI and compared it with the track (`07_documentation/agent_workflows/verification_scripts/R2-figures-features/`). All counts above were recomputed in the code pass with interval arithmetic that shares nothing with the pipeline's helpers (scripts in the session's scratch directory, not in the repository). No pipeline step writes them.

**Options (as put to the author on 2026-11-19).** One of two. (1) Keep the track and call it what it is in Methods 2.3, Results 3.1 and 3.3 and Figure 3A: repeats (RepeatMasker, all classes). No number changes. (2) Restrict it to transposable elements: rebuild the track from the RepeatMasker output with a filter on the repeat class (column 11; say whether the class unknown, 9,973 records, counts) and with begin minus 1 as the BED start. The percentages of Results 3.1 and 3.3 and Figure 3A then change as given above, and legacy mode would no longer reproduce the draft's numbers for this track, so this needs the author's approval.

**Verification 2026-11-19 (main session).** The main session recounted the numbers of this entry independently, and they hold (`07_documentation/agent_workflows/verification_scripts/main-session-repeat-track/`, `README_check.txt` and `te_classes_check.R`): the track is columns 5 to 7 of the RepeatMasker output line for line; the records by class; the 1-based coordinates; and, in legacy mode, 109,966 analysed CpGs, 194 ploidy DML, 463 heat DML and 28 loci in both lists as the draft read the file, 110,067, 194, 465 and 28 with the coordinates as written, and 106,269, 189, 455 and 28 with the five transposable element classes alone. It adds that of the 3,697 analysed CpGs of the track outside the classes DNA, RC, LTR, LINE and SINE, 2,158 lie in records of class Unknown and 1,539 in simple repeats, low complexity, satellites or rRNA alone (of the 5 ploidy DML outside the five classes, 3 and 2; of the 8 heat DML, 5 and 3). NCBI's record of the run (`GCF_902806645.1_cgigas_uk_roslin_v1_rm.run`, read 2026-11-19) gives RepeatMasker open-4.0.8 in sensitive mode, the combined library Dfam_Consensus-20181026 and RepBase-20181026, and the species crassostrea gigas; the run has no option that leaves simple repeats or low complexity out.

**Decision of the author (2026-11-20).** Option 1: the track keeps its content and its numbers, nothing is recomputed, and the category is called "repetitive elements (RepeatMasker, all classes)" in Methods 2.3, Results 3.1 and 3.3 and Figure 3A, with one sentence on its composition (`07_documentation/reproducibility_plan.md`, table "Decided"). Legacy mode therefore keeps reproducing the draft's numbers for this track. A track restricted to the transposable element classes is not part of the decision and is not built.

**Manuscript text (2026-11-20).** The session placed seven suggested edits in the Google Doc of the draft, in suggestion mode; none is applied until an author accepts it. Methods 2.3 now reads "The locations of repetitive elements (RepeatMasker, all classes) were obtained from the RepeatMasker output of the NCBI RefSeq annotation", followed by a new sentence: "This track holds every repeat class that RepeatMasker reports: transposable elements (DNA transposons, Helitrons, LINE, LTR and SINE elements; 74.5% of records and 94.5% of annotated bases), simple repeats, low-complexity sequence, satellites and rRNA (24.2% of records and 4.0% of annotated bases), and unclassified repeats (1.3% of records and 1.5% of annotated bases)". The shares come from the class table of `README_check.txt`: 582,403, 188,936 and 9,973 of the 781,312 records, and 228,359,255, 9,542,929 and 3,641,919 of the 241,544,103 bases summed over records. The five occurrences of "transposable elements" in Results 3.1 and 3.3 became "repetitive elements", the first two as "repetitive elements (RepeatMasker, all classes; 13.1%)" and "(RepeatMasker, all classes; 18.4%)"; with the one in Methods 2.3 they were the six occurrences of the term in the document. No number of the draft changes. Figure 3A inside the document is an image, which the authors have to replace with the regenerated figure.

**Still to do in the pipeline** (not done when this entry was written): the label of the track in step 07 and in `feature_track_labels()` of `02_R_functions/genomic_overlaps.R`, Figure 3A, the README files, and the quotes of the values-map rows listed under Where. The key `TE` stays, because the diagnostics and the values map select the track by it.

**Update 2026-11-20 (pipeline relabelled).** The label is applied: column `track_label` of `04_output/07_genome_features/tables/feature_overlap_by_track_<mode>.csv` and `feature_association_tests_<mode>.csv` and of `04_output/04_cpg_coverage/tables/cpg_counts_by_feature_track.csv` now reads "repetitive elements (RepeatMasker, all classes; 1-based coordinates read as BED)" (`feature_track_labels()` in `02_R_functions/genomic_overlaps.R`), and the READMEs and the notes of nine rows of the values map say the same. The track code `TE` and the column `pct_in_TE` are kept, so no expression changed; only label columns differ from the previous tables, step 13 still gives 352 matches, 146 differences and 47 values not regenerated, and the legacy check 329 of 331. The pipeline's Figure 3A draws exclusive classes and has no repeat category, so it has no label to change; the repeat bar of the draft's Figure 3A is in the authors' image. The `quote` and `item` columns of the values map keep the draft's wording until the authors accept the suggested edits. Three diagnostics (`feature_odds_matched.R`, `locus_nc047563.R`, `coverage_filter_variants.R`) still say "transposable elements" in comments and descriptions.
